Colimits of categories, zig-zags and necklaces
Redi Haderi

Abstract
Given a diagram of small categories F : 𝒥 → Cat F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat} , we provide a combinatorial description of its colimit in terms of the indexing category 𝒥 \mathcal{J} and the categories and functors in the diagram F F . We introduce certain double categories of zig-zags in order to keep track of the necessary identifications. We found these double categories necessary, but also explanatory. When applied pointwise in the simplicially enriched setting, our constructions offer a shorter proof of the necklace theorem of Dugger and Spivak by direct computation.



1
Introduction and summary

This note was originally intended as an appendix in another project, but grew as an independent work. The motivating objects of study are small diagrams

F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}

in the category of simplicially enriched categories.
The colimits of such diagrams are generally deemed hard to understand. Important constructions, such as the free simplicially enriched category
\mathfrak{C}X
generated by a simplicial set
X
, arise as such colimits.

Colimits of simplicial categories

Let
\mathcal{C}\in\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}
. Given objects
a,b\in\mathcal{C}
and
[p]\in\Delta
, we refer to a
p
-simplex
u\in\mathcal{C}(a,b)_{p}
in the mapping space as a
p
-
arrow
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{p}
be the category whose objects are those of
\mathcal{C}
and morphisms are
p
-arrows.

The assignment
[p]\mapsto\mathcal{C}_{p}
creates a simplicial object in
Cat

\mathcal{C}_{*}:\Delta^{op}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}

which, in turn, produces a functor

(-)_{*}:\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}^{\Delta^{op}}

The functor
(-)_{*}
is a fully faithful embedding. Its image consists of
discrete
simplicial objects in
Cat
: those objects
\mathcal{D}\in\textbf{Cat}^{\Delta^{op}}
for which the categories
\mathcal{D}_{p}
have the same objects for all
[p]
, and such that the functors
\theta^{*}:\mathcal{D}_{p}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{q}
are identity on objects for all morphisms
\theta:[q]\rightarrow[p]
in
\Delta
.

The functor
(-)_{*}
admits a right adjoint

(-)_{*}:\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}\leftrightarrows\textbf{Cat}^{\Delta^{op}}:(-)_{v}

The functor
(-)_{v}
assigns to a simplicial object
\mathcal{D}
its
vertical
1
1

1

This term is adopted from double category theory, in an analogy developed in
[
3
]
.
simplicial category
\mathcal{D}_{v}
. Roughly speaking,
\mathcal{D}_{v}
is the subobject of
\mathcal{D}
spanned by totally degenerate objects of
\mathcal{D}_{p}
for
[p]\in\Delta
, or equivalently the largest discrete subobject of
\mathcal{D}
.

In light of the above, given a diagram

F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}

of simplicial categories we will have

(\colim_{i\in\mathcal{J}}F(i))_{p}=\colim_{i\in\mathcal{J}}F(i)_{p}

for all
[p]\in\Delta
. Hence, the problem of understanding the left-hand side reduces to understanding the right-hand side, which is a colimit in
Cat
.

Colimits of categories

We turn our attention to (small) diagrams of (small) categories

F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}

For practical ease, we adopt the following notation for the rest of this paper:

\begin{matrix}F:&i&\mapsto&\mathcal{C}_{i}\\
&u:i\rightarrow j&\mapsto&\tilde{u}:\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{j}\end{matrix}

Let
\mathcal{C}=\colim\limits_{i\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{C}_{i}

In some sense, there is no problem in constructing the colimit
\mathcal{C}
. For example, utilizing the free category-nerve adjunction
\tau:\textbf{sSet}\leftrightarrows\textbf{Cat}:N
, we may derive that
\mathcal{C}\cong\tau(\colim\limits_{i\in\mathcal{J}}N(\mathcal{C}_{i}))
(for instance, see
[
6
, Corollary 4.5.16]
).

While we may use the above observation to imply the existence of
\mathcal{C}
, its structure remains
implicit
. On objects, given that the functor
Ob:\textbf{Cat}\rightarrow\textbf{Set}
, which assigns to a small category its set of objects, has a right adjoint, we may conclude that

Ob(\mathcal{C})\cong\colim_{i\in\mathcal{J}}Ob(C_{i})

The right-hand side is well-understood as a colimit in
Set
. This way, the objects of
\mathcal{C}
are equivalence classes
[a]
represented by objects
a\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
i\in\mathcal{J}
.

The problem is in describing the morphism sets
\mathcal{C}([a],[b])
given two objects
[a],[b]\in\mathcal{C}
. An explicit description would have to be in terms on the objects and morphisms of the categories
\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
i\in\mathcal{J}
, and the diagram
F
.

Taking a step back, observe that, in constructing
\mathcal{C}
, for a morphism
u:i\rightarrow j
in
\mathcal{J}
and a morphism
f:a\rightarrow b
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, we ought to identify
a\sim\tilde{u}(a)
,
b\sim\tilde{u}(b)
and
f\sim\tilde{u}(f)
.
These identifications create the following basic type of situation. In case of a
roof
in
\mathcal{J}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

consider objects
a\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}_{j}
,
c\in\mathcal{C}_{k}
and morphisms
f:a\rightarrow\tilde{l}(c)
,
g:\tilde{r}(c)\rightarrow b
a
\tilde{l}(c)
\tilde{r}(c)
b
c
f
g

After the identifications, the arrows
f
and
g
become composable in
\mathcal{C}
and create a new morphism
(f,g)
.

General morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
are simply such sequences, except possibly longer and indexed by
zig-zags
in
\mathcal{J}
rather than mere roofs. This idea is formulated in the following theorem, which is our first contribution.

Theorem 1.1
.

With notation as above, an element of the set
\mathcal{C}([a],[b])
is represented by the following three pieces of data:

(i)

A choice of representatives
a\in C_{i}
and
b\in C_{j}
for some
i,j\in\mathcal{J}
.

(ii)

A zig-zag in
\mathcal{J}
which connects
i
and
j

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i=i_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{n}=j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{2}}

(iii)

A sequence of objects
a_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{k}}
and a “chain” of morphisms
f_{k}\in C_{i_{k}}
of the following form:

a\xrightarrow{f_{0}}\tilde{l}_{1}(a_{1})\ ,\ \tilde{r}_{1}(a_{1})\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\tilde{l}_{2}(a_{2})\ ,\dots,\ \tilde{r}_{n}(a_{n})\xrightarrow{f_{n}}b

subject to a relation described in Section
3.3
.

After our thought experiment, the data postulated in the above theorem is intuitive and not difficult to organize and keep track of. For instance, we regard zig-zags as in (ii) as a notion of morphism on the objects of
\mathcal{J}
, depicted as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r})}

Composing by concatenation, we form the category of zig-zags
\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{J}
.

We refer to a chain of morphisms as in (iii) as an
F
-
decoration
of the zig-zag
(\bar{l},\bar{r})
. Such a decorated zig-zag can also be thought of as a morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f})}

in a category
\mathcal{Z}F
whose set of objects is
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{J}}Ob(\mathcal{C}_{i})
.

To address the challenging bit of this line of thought, which is keeping track of the necessary relations, we resort to an excellent organizing structure:
double categories
. We construct double categories
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
and
\mathbb{Z}F
whose 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}}

encode the appropriate notion of transformation between zig-zags and their decorations. These double categories will be introduced in Section
3.2
.

Reinterpreting the necklace theorem

Let
X
be a simplicial set. It is of interest in higher category theory to understand the free simplicial category
\mathfrak{C}X
generated by
X
. The functor

\mathfrak{C}:\textbf{sSet}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}

is the left adjoint of the coherent nerve functor, and this pair has been shown to be a Quillen equivalence between the Joyal model structure on
sSet
and the Bergner model structure on
\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}
(
[
1
]
) by Lurie (
[
5
]
). In case
X
is an
\infty
-category (i.e. quasi-category), the simplicial category
\mathfrak{C}X
may be interpreted as a strictification of some of the compositions in
X
. In case
X
is (the nerve of) a category,
\mathfrak{C}X
provides a cofibrant replacement in the Bergner model structure (
X
being regarded as simplicially enriched by discrete simplicial sets).

In the case
X=\Delta^{n}
, we denote
\mathfrak{C}\Delta^{n}=\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}
. For
[p]\in\Delta
, the category of
p
-arrows
\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}_{p}
may be combinatorially described as follows:

(\bullet)

The objects are
0,1,\dots,n
.

(\rightarrow)

For two objects
i,j
, a
p
-arrow
\overrightarrow{U}:i\rightarrow j
is a
flag

U^{0}\subseteq U^{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq U^{p}

of subsets of
\{i,\dots,j\}
such that
i,j\in U^{0}
.

(\circ)

Composition is given by union of sets.

For a morphism
\theta:[q]\rightarrow[p]
in
\Delta
, the identity on objects functor
\theta^{*}:\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}_{q}
is given, for a morphism
\overrightarrow{U}:i\rightarrow j
, by

\theta^{*}(\overrightarrow{U})=(U^{\theta(0)}\subseteq\dots\subseteq U^{\theta(p)})

In particular, faces and degeneracies are obtained by deletion and repetition of terms in the flag
\overrightarrow{U}
.

We extend the free simplicial category functor
\mathfrak{C}
from
\Delta^{n}
to general simplicial sets via Yoneda extension. This means the following. For a simplicial set
X
, let
\mathcal{S}(X)
denote its category of simplices, given by:

(\bullet)

Objects the simplices
x\in X_{n}
for
[n]\in\Delta
.

(\rightarrow)

Morphisms
x\rightarrow y
commutative triangles of simplicial maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

The assignment
(x\in X_{n})\mapsto\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}
gives rise to a diagram of simplicial categories

\chi:\mathcal{S}(X)\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}_{\Delta}

We define
\mathfrak{C}X
to be the colimit of this functor, typically expressed as

\mathfrak{C}X\cong\colim_{x\in X_{n}}\mathbb{\Delta}^{n}

The objects of
\mathfrak{C}X
are the
0
-simplices of
X
. Given
a,b\in X_{0}
and
[p]\in\Delta
, a combinatorial description of the
p
-arrows in
\mathfrak{C}X(a,b)_{p}
, which extends the one presented above for the case
X=\Delta^{n}
, has been provided by Dugger and Spivak in
[
2
]
. We briefly summarize.

Let
\textbf{sSet}_{*,*}
be the category of bipointed simplicial sets. We regard
\Delta^{n}\in\textbf{sSet}_{*,*}
with chosen basepoints
0
and
n
. The wedge sum
\Delta^{n}\vee\Delta^{m}
is provided by stringing
\Delta^{n}
and
\Delta^{m}
along the endpoint
n
of
\Delta^{n}
and
0
of
\Delta^{m}
.
A
necklace
is a simplicial set of the form

N=\Delta^{n_{0}}\vee\dots\vee\Delta^{n_{k}}

For a necklace
N
as above define the following:

-

(Basepoints).
N
is regarded as bipointed by the initial vertex of
\Delta^{n_{0}}
and the terminal vertex of
\Delta^{n_{k}}
.

-

(Beads). The beads of the necklace
N
are the simplicial sets
\Delta^{n_{i}}
,
0\leq i\leq k
.

-

(Joins). The set
J_{N}
of joins of the necklace
N
is the union of basepoints of the beads (with the identifications provided by wedging).

-

(Vertices). The set
V_{N}
of vertices of the necklace
N
is the union of the vertices of the beads (with the identifications provided by wedging).

Theorem 1.2

(Dugger and Spivak, Theorem 1.4
[
2
]
)
.

Let
X
be a simplicial set,
a,b\in X_{0}
and
[p]\in\Delta
. An element of the
p
-arrow set
\mathfrak{C}X(a,b)_{p}
is represented by a pair
(N\rightarrow X_{a,b},\overrightarrow{U})
where

(i)

N
is a necklace and
N\rightarrow X_{a,b}
is a map of bipointed simplicial sets.

(ii)

\overrightarrow{U}
is a flag

U^{0}\subseteq U^{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq U^{p}

of subsets of
V_{N}
such that
J_{N}\subseteq U^{0}
.

Two such pairs
(N\rightarrow X_{a,b},\overrightarrow{U})
and
(M\rightarrow X_{a,b},\overrightarrow{V})
are identified in case there is a commutative triangle of bipointed maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{a,b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

such that
f(\overrightarrow{U})=\overrightarrow{V}
.

In light of our previous observations, for
[p]\in\Delta
, the category of
p
-arrows
(\mathfrak{C}X)_{p}
arises as the colimit of the diagram

\begin{matrix}\chi_{p}:&\mathcal{S}(X)&\rightarrow&\textbf{Cat}\\
&x\in X_{n}&\mapsto&\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}_{p}\end{matrix}

Therefore, we can implement our colimit theorem and understand the necklace theorem as follows:

(A)

The packet of data
(N\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U})
representing a morphism
a\rightarrow b
in
\mathfrak{C}X(a,b)_{p}
can be interpreted as:

-

A choice of simplices
x
and
y
in
X
such that
a
is the initial vertex of
x
and
b
is the terminal vertex of
y
.

-

A zig-zag
x\rightsquigarrow y
in
\mathcal{S}(X)
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x=x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k}=y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}

where
a_{i}
’s are vertices of
X
and
\alpha
and
\omega
denote initial and terminal vertex inclusions into simplices.

-

A chain of
p
-arrows
\overrightarrow{U}_{1}:a\rightarrow a_{1},\dots,\overrightarrow{U}_{k}:a_{n-1}\rightarrow b
, obtained by splitting the sets in
\overrightarrow{U}
according to the joins of
N
.

(B)

On the other hand, our colimit theorem states that a
p
-arrow
a\rightarrow b
in
\mathfrak{C}X(a,b)_{p}
is, in general, represented by

-

A choice of simplices
x,y\in X
such that
a
is a vertex of
x
and
b
a vertex of
y
.

-

A connecting zig-zag between
x\rightsquigarrow y
in
\mathcal{S}(X)
, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x=x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k}=y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}

-

A choice of vertices
a_{i}
in
x_{i}
and a chain
\overrightarrow{U}_{1}:a\rightarrow a_{1},\overrightarrow{U}_{2}:a_{1}\rightarrow a_{2},\dots,\overrightarrow{U}_{n}:a_{n}\rightarrow b
, where
\overrightarrow{U}_{i}
is a
p
-arrow in the category
\mathbb{\Delta^{n_{i}}}_{p}
corresponding to the simplex
x_{i}:\Delta^{n_{i}}\rightarrow X
.

(C)

Upon scrutiny, data as in (B) can always be replaced with data as in (A). For instance, in forming the
\chi_{p}
-decoration in (B) we are not making use of the simplices
y_{i}
except in choosing the vertices
a_{i}
. Such a replacement of data can be carried out systematically, i.e. functorially. We present this in Construction
4.1
and label it
necklace replacement
.

(D)

In the case of data as in (A), the relation between decorated zig-zags used in the colimit theorem translates, on the nose, to the relation in the necklace theorem. This is part of the content of Proposition
4.1
.

We could consider the above steps as a proof of the necklace theorem by
direct computation
.
We find our approach to have three advantages:

-

The proofs, in our opinion, seem to be more transparent as a result of the explicit nature of our colimit theorem.

-

We provide an explanation of why necklaces appear in the first place in a combinatorial description of
\mathfrak{C}X
. They appear as a natural simplification of general zig-zags which appear in colimits of categories. Another way to put this is to say that necklaces in
X
are a subclass of zig-zags in
\mathcal{S}(X)
sufficient to record all
\chi_{p}
-decorations in forming the colimit
\mathfrak{C}X
.

-

We provide an explanation of why the relation between the data in the necklace theorem is what it is. It is a translation in the language of simplicial sets of relations between zig-zags which appear in studying colimits of categories. Moreover, the latter are natural and sensible in their own right.

Ultimately, we let the reader be the judge. This paper represents our attempt in understanding some of the concepts involved in these results.

Conventions

All categories which appear in this paper are assumed to be small. We have assumed some familiarity with simplicial sets, and used fairly standard notation. Background on simplicial sets is only necessary for the parts of the paper dedicated to necklaces though.

Note

As far as we know, our colimit theorem for small categories does not appear in the literature in the form presented here, nor do our double categories of zig-zags or our proof of the necklace theorem.

We apologize in advance for any missing reference, and would be more than happy to retract any claim of originality and include relevant references in future versions of this paper.

2
Zig-zags and their origins

Let

\begin{matrix}F:&\mathcal{J}&\rightarrow&\textbf{Set}\\
&i&\mapsto&C_{i}\\
&i\xrightarrow{u}j&\mapsto&C_{i}\xrightarrow{\tilde{u}}C_{j}\end{matrix}

be a diagram of sets. An easy exercise in category theory reveals that the colimit of the diagram
F
is the quotient of the set

\displaystyle DC

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{J}}C_{i}

\displaystyle=\{(i,a)\ :\ i\in\mathcal{J},a\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\}

by the following relation:

(E)

A pair of elements
(i,a)
and
(j,b)
are in the relation
E
if there is a morphism
u:i\rightarrow j
in
\mathcal{J}
such that
\tilde{u}(a)=b
.

We would like to emphasize a couple of ideas which are implicitly present in the formation of colimits of sets.

The relation
E
is naturally recorded by a notion of morphism on the data set
DC
, by regarding a witness
u
in the relation as a morphism
(i,a)\rightarrow(j,b)
. This is the well-known
category of elements

\mathcal{E}(F)
associated to
F
. In fact, we have

C\cong\pi_{0}\mathcal{E}(F)

where
\pi_{0}:\textbf{Cat}\rightarrow\textbf{Set}
is the connected components functor.

The second type structure brought to attention by colimits of sets,
\pi_{0}
being involved, is
zig-zags
. In general, two objects of a category
\mathcal{A}
represent the same class in
\pi_{0}\mathcal{A}
if and only if there is a zig-zag of morphisms connecting them. We record a little bit of zig-zag formalism.

For
n\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{Z}^{n}
be the walking zig-zag of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{n}}

obtained by stringing
n
roofs along endpoints.
The sequences of morphisms
\bar{R}
and
\bar{L}
are the morphisms pointing to the left and right respectively. In case
n=0
, we let
\mathcal{Z}^{0}=1
be the terminal category.

For a category
\mathcal{J}
, we define a zig-zag in
\mathcal{J}
to be a functor

(\bar{l},\bar{r}):\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}

The notation
(\bar{l},\bar{r})
indicates that the zig-zag is comprised of left pointing arrows
l_{1},\dots,l_{n}
and right pointing arrows
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}
. In case
n=0
, a functor
\mathcal{Z}^{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
picks an object
i\in\mathcal{J}
. We denote this zig-zag
\{i\}
and refer to it as the
trivial
zig-zag at
i
.

We say that a zig-zag
\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}

connects
two objects
i
and
j
in case
t_{0}\mapsto i
and
t_{n}\mapsto j
. For emphasis, we write
\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}_{i,j}
. In fact, we regard
\mathcal{Z}^{n}
as a category with two chosen basepoints
t_{0}
and
t_{n}
. The category of small categories with two chosen basepoints and functors which preserves them is denoted
\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}
. This way
\mathcal{J}_{i,j}\in\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}
with basepoints
i
and
j
and zig-zags connecting
i
and
j
are functors in
\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}
.
We depict such zig-zags with curly arrows

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r})}

Being bipointed categories, we have a wedging operation on walking zig-zags (which strings two zig-zags together), so that

\mathcal{Z}^{n}\vee\mathcal{Z}^{m}\cong\mathcal{Z}^{n+m}

This operation induces the
concatenation
operation on zig-zags in
\mathcal{J}
. We obtain a category, denoted
\mathcal{ZJ}
, whose objects are those of
\mathcal{J}
and morphisms are zig-zags.

Definition 2.1

(
F
-decoration)
.

Let
F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Set}
be a diagram and
(\bar{l},\bar{r}):i\rightsquigarrow j
be a zig-zag, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i=i_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{n}=j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{n}}

In notation as above, an
F
-decoration of
(\bar{l},\bar{r})
consists of a sequence
\bar{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1})
, where
a_{k}\in C_{j_{k}}
and such that
\tilde{r}_{k}(a_{k})=\tilde{l}_{k+1}(a_{k+1})
for all
k
.

Given two elements
(i,a),(j,b)\in DC
, we write

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{a})}

to indicate an
F
-decorated zig-zag as in the above definition which connects
a
and
b
, i.e. such that
\tilde{l}_{1}(a_{1})=a
and
\tilde{r}_{n}(a_{n})=b
. By concatenating in the evident manner, we obtain a category
\mathcal{Z}F
with set of objects
DC
.

To obtain the colimit of
F
, we may form the quotient of
DC
by the following relation:

(Z)

A pair of elements
(i,a)
and
(j,b)
are in the relation
Z
if there is an
F
-decorated zig-zag
(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(j,b)
connecting them.

With respect to
E
, the relation
Z
has the advantage of being an equivalence relation. Moreover, it is more explicit and, arguably, explanatory.

Remark
.

The category
\mathcal{Z}F
defined above is just the category of zig-zags
\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{E}(F)
associated to the category of elements. While we could have defined
\mathcal{Z}F
this way, we intended to emphasize the notion of
F
-decoration, a categorified version of which will be presented in the next section.

3
Colimits of small categories

We saw how, in studying colimits of sets, the relevant relations are best organized and presented in terms of certain categories. In studying colimits of small categories, we find that the relevant relations are best presented in terms of certain
double categories
. We briefly review the latter and present an explicit construction of colimits in
Cat
. This challenge is mostly about book-keeping of indices and notation.

3.1
Two types of arrows? No problem.

Definition 3.1

(Double category)
.

A double category is a category object in
Cat
.

In order to observe double categories in nature, we ought to unpack the data encoded in the definition in terms of combinatorial elements and possibly depict the latter. Let
\mathbb{D}
be a double category. The first piece of data encoded in
\mathbb{D}
consists of two categories
\mathbb{D}_{0}
and
\mathbb{D}_{1}
related by source and target functors

s,t:\mathbb{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{0}

We interpret this data as follows:

(\bullet)

The
objects
of
\mathbb{D}
are the objects of the category
\mathbb{D}_{0}
.

(\downarrow)

The morphisms in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
are the
vertical
morphisms in
\mathbb{D}
.

({\mathrel{\to}})

The objects of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, being equipped with a source and target in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
, serve as another notion of morphism between objects of
\mathbb{D}
which we call
horizontal
morphisms. In order to distinguish the latter from vertical arrows, we depict them as
{\mathrel{\to}}
.

(\square)

A morphism in
\mathbb{D}_{1}
, which we write as a double arrow
\rho:u\Rightarrow v
, is a square-shaped
2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

whose boundary is comprised of vertical and horizontal arrows. A cell as above records the fact that
s(\rho)=f
and
t(\rho)=g
.

We have two types of composition of arrows and 2-cells: vertical and horizontal. Vertical arrows compose as prescribed in
\mathbb{D}_{0}
. Moreover, 2-cells compose vertically as prescribed by
\mathbb{D}_{1}
:

\mathbb{D}
is provided by the composition functor

\otimes:\mathbb{D}_{1}\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{D}_{1}

which is part of the data for
\mathbb{D}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.83192pt}{-1.40276pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

These compositions are
associative
and
unital
and they satisfy an
interchange law
similar to that of 2-categories (which encodes the functoriallity of
\otimes
).

We see from the above description, that double categories arise when we have two notions of morphism between a given class of objects. As the latter happens a lot, there are many interesting examples which we leave to the reader to explore. Recently, the literature on double categories has grown significantly.

We also see that the definition of double category is symmetric with respect to the vertical and horizontal direction. However, as our notation suggests, our preference is to reserve the vertical direction for “traditional” morphisms and let the horizontal direction record “exotic” types of morphism (such as zig-zags for instance).

3.2
Double categories of zig-zags

Let
\mathcal{J}
be a category. We may form a double category
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
whose objects and vertical morphisms are those of
\mathcal{J}
, while horizontal morphisms are zig-zags in
\mathcal{J}
. A 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where
(\bar{l},\bar{r}):\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
and
(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime}):\mathcal{Z}^{m}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
,
consists of:

-

A functor
\theta:\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{m}
which preserves the endpoints (the first and last target objects).

-

A natural transformation
\rho:(\bar{l},\bar{r})\Rightarrow(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime})\theta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}^{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

We can describe 2-cells in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
in more explicit terms.

Lemma 3.1
.

There is a bijection

\textbf{Cat}_{*,*}(\mathcal{Z}^{n},\mathcal{Z}^{m})\cong\textbf{Surj}([n],[m])

where the set on the right has as elements order preserving surjections
[n]\rightarrow[m]
in
\Delta
.

Proof.

This is easy to see, since we have

\mathcal{Z}^{n}\cong\underbrace{\mathcal{Z}^{1}\vee\dots\vee\mathcal{Z}^{1}}_{n-\text{times}}

In creating a functor
\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{m}
which preserves endpoints, for the first copy of
\mathcal{Z}^{1}
we have two choices: either map it to the first endpoint of
\mathcal{Z}^{m}
or identically to the first copy of
\mathcal{Z}^{1}
in
\mathcal{Z}^{m}
. And so on for the rest.
∎

In light of the above lemma, the 2-cells in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
are generated by concatenation (horizontal composition) by the following two types of cells:

(i)

Cells indexed by the map
s^{0}:[1]\rightarrow[0]
, which correspond to commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}}

Such a 2-cell has horizontal source
(l,r)
and target the trivial zig-zag
\{k\}
.

(ii)

Cells indexed by the identity
id:[1]\rightarrow[1]
, which are simply commutative diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}}

Such a 2-cell has horizontal source
(l,r)
and horizontal target
(l^{\prime},r^{\prime})
.

Our second example of double category is a decorated version of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
.

Definition 3.2

(
F
-decoration)
.

Let
F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}
be a diagram of small categories and
(\bar{l},\bar{r}):i\rightsquigarrow j
be a zig-zag, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i=i_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i_{n}=j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{n}}

An
F
-decoration of
(\bar{l},\bar{r})
consists of sequence of objects
a_{k}\in\mathcal{C}_{j_{k}}
,
k=1,\dots,n
, and a chain of morphisms

a\xrightarrow{f_{0}}\tilde{l}_{1}(a_{1})\ ,\ \tilde{r}_{1}(a_{1})\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\tilde{l}_{2}(a_{2})\ ,\dots,\ \tilde{r}_{n}(a_{n})\xrightarrow{f_{n}}b

An
F
-decoration of the trivial zig-zag
\{i\}
consists of a morphism
a\xrightarrow{f}b
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

Let
DC=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{J}}Ob(\mathcal{C}_{i})
.
F
-decorated zig-zags provide a notion of morphism between elements of
DC
, as we regard an
F
-decorated zig-zag as in the above definition to be an arrow

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f})}

The composite
(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(j,b)\rightsquigarrow(k,c)
is provided by concatenation of zig-zags in
\mathcal{J}
and on decorations, by composing in
\mathcal{C}_{j}
the last morphism in the first chain with the first morphisms in the second chain and then concatenating. The identity morphism on
(i,a)
is provided by decorating the trivial zig-zag
\{i\}
with the identity morphism
id_{a}
.

The double category
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
acts on
F
-decorated zig-zags as follows. Given a 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

and a decoration
(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f}):(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(j,b)
, there is a decoration

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime},\rho^{*}\bar{f})}

where
a^{\prime}=\tilde{\rho}_{i}(a)
and
b^{\prime}=\tilde{\rho}_{j}(b)
. The chain
\rho^{*}\bar{f}
is defined, roughly speaking, by pushing down the chain
\bar{f}
along the components of
\rho
and then composing as prescribed by the map
\theta
in
\Delta
which indexes
\rho
.

More precisely, the action of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
is defined on generating 2-cells as follows.

(i)

In case the 2-cell
\rho
is a commutative square,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}}

given a decoration
a\xrightarrow{f_{0}}\tilde{l}(a_{1}),\tilde{r}(a_{1})\xrightarrow{f_{1}}b
, we define
\rho^{*}\bar{f}
to be the decoration on the trivial zig-zag
\{k\}
provided by the composite

\rho^{*}\bar{f}:a^{\prime}=\tilde{\rho}_{1}(a)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\rho}_{i}f_{0}}\widetilde{\rho_{i}l}(a_{1})=\widetilde{\rho_{j}r}(a_{1})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\rho}_{j}f_{1}}\tilde{\rho}_{j}(b)=b^{\prime}

(ii)

In case
\rho
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}}

we simply define
\rho^{*}(f_{0},f_{1})=(\tilde{\rho}_{i}f_{0},\tilde{\rho}_{j}f_{1})

We extend the above action for general
\rho
by concatenation in the horizontal direction. This is clearly functorial with respect to vertical composition of 2-cells in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
, so that the term “action” is justified (see also the remark below).

Lastly, we form a double category
\mathbb{Z}F
with:

(\bullet)

Set of objects
DC=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{J}}Ob(\mathcal{C}_{i})
.

(\rightsquigarrow)

Horizontal morphisms
F
-decorated zig-zags
(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(j,b)
.

(\downarrow)

Vertical morphisms
u^{*}:(i,a)\rightarrow(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
provided by morphisms
u:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
such that
\tilde{u}(a)=a^{\prime}
.

(\square)

2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime},\bar{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}}

provided by 2-cells
\rho
in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
such that
\rho^{*}\bar{f}=\bar{g}
.

Note

(Functorial relations)
.

At this point we can intuit how the double category
\mathbb{Z}F
records the necessary relations for us to extract the colimit of
F
. For instance, its vertical category is precisely the category of elements
\mathcal{E}(ObF)
of the restriction of
F
on objects, and hence records the correct identifications on objects. The 2-cells record the identifications on morphisms in a manner which is
functorial
with respect to the identifications on objects.

Remark

(Double fibrations)
.

There is a forgetful double functor
\mathbb{Z}F\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
. This functor is an example of a
discrete double fibration
. The latter are simply category objects in the category of discrete fibrations and have been studied in
[
4
]
. In this vein, a 2-cell
\rho^{*}
in
\mathbb{Z}F
is the cocartesian lift of the corresponding 2-cell
\rho
in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
. Such fibrations correspond to double functors into a suitable double category of sets (see
[
4
]
).

We are ultimately interested in encoding relations via double categories. Just as in the case of relations encoded by categories, they suffer from a lack of symmetry. For a category
\mathcal{E}
, this is resolved by forming the category of zig-zags
\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{E}
. A similar construction is possible for double categories.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a double category. There is a double category
\mathcal{Z}\mathbb{D}
with

(\mathcal{Z}\mathbb{D})_{0}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{D}_{0})\ \ ,\ \ (\mathcal{Z}\mathbb{D})_{1}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{D}_{1})

In other words, 2-cells in
\mathcal{Z}\mathbb{D}
are zig-zags (in the vertical direction) of 2-cells in
\mathbb{D}
. For instance,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

In this double category, vertical composition in provided by concatenation, while horizontal composition is provided by composing in
\mathbb{D}
.

We are interested in the double categories

\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J})\ \ ,\ \ \tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{Z}F)

In
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}
, both horizontal and vertical morphisms are zig-zags in
\mathcal{J}
. In
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F
, the horizontal morphisms are
F
-decorated zig-zags, while the vertical morphisms are zig-zags in
\mathcal{E}(ObF)
. We regard the latter as
F
-decorated by identities. These double categories are well-behaved and have pleasant properties which we discuss in Appendix
A.1
.

Remark
.

We still have a forgetful functor
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F\rightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}
, but this functor fails to be a fibration, so we cannot speak of an action.

3.3
A model for colimits

For a diagram of categories

F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}

we construct an explicit category
\mathcal{C}
and demonstrate that it serves as a colimit for
F
.

(\bullet)

We define

Ob(\mathcal{C})=\colim_{i\in\mathcal{J}}Ob(\mathcal{C}_{i})

Hence, objects are represented by pairs
(i,a)\in DC
subject to the relation provided by morphisms in the category of elements
\mathcal{E}(ObF)
.

(\rightarrow)

Let
[a],[b]\in Ob(\mathcal{C})
. A morphism
[a]\rightarrow[b]
in
\mathcal{C}
consists of an
F
-decorated zig-zag

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f})}

where
(i,a)
and
(j,b)
are representatives of
[a]
and
[b]
, subject to the relation determined by 2-cells in the double category
\mathbb{Z}F
. In other words, in case there is a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime},\bar{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}}

the top and bottom zig-zags represent the same morphism
[a]\rightarrow[b]
.

(\circ)

Let
[a]\rightarrow[b]
and
[b]\rightarrow[c]
be composable morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. Assume these morphisms are represented by
F
-decorated zig-zags
(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(j,b)
and
(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})\rightsquigarrow(k,c)
. Choose an
ObF
-decorated zig-zag
(j,b)\rightsquigarrow(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})
(we know there exists at least one such zig-zag), which we regard as
F
-decorated by identity morphisms.

We define the composite
[a]\rightarrow[c]
to be represented by the composite of
F
-decorated zig-zags

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,k)}}

(=)

For
[a]\in\mathcal{C}
, we choose a representative
(i,a)
and define the identity morphism
id_{[a]}
to be represented by
(\{i\},id_{a})
.

Proposition 3.1
.

As defined above, the category
\mathcal{C}
is well-defined.

The proof of this proposition relies on moving zig-zags around. We present it systematically in Appendix
A.2
.

Theorem 3.1
.

There is an isomorphism

\mathcal{C}\cong\colim_{i\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{C}_{i}

Proof.

We demonstrate that
\mathcal{C}
has the structure of a cocone over
F
by constructing a natural transformation
\phi:F\Rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and then verify that this cocone is universal.

For
i\in\mathcal{J}
, let the functor

\phi_{i}:\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

be defined by
\phi_{i}(a)=[a]
on objects. For a morphism
f:a\rightarrow b
, we let
\phi_{i}(f)
be the morphism in
\mathcal{C}
represented by the decoration
(\{i\},f):(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(i,b)
on the trivial zig-zag
\{i\}
by the morphism
f
.

For a morphism
u:i\rightarrow j
in
\mathcal{J}
, the triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{u}}

commutes. This is clear on objects. For a morphism
f
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, the morphism
u
, regarded an identity 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
, induces a 2-cell
u^{*}:(\{i\},f)\rightarrow(\{j\},\tilde{u}(f))
in
\mathbb{Z}F
which identifies its source and target in
\mathcal{C}
.

To verify the universal property, let
\mathcal{D}
be a category with the structure of a cocone
\psi:F\Rightarrow\mathcal{D}
. We construct a functor
H:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
which makes the following triangle commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

On objects, given
[a]\in\mathcal{C}
, we choose a representative
(i,a)
and define

H([a])=\psi_{i}(a)

This is well-defined, since any two representatives are connected by an
ObF
-decorated zig-zag which maps under
\psi
to a chain of identity morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
.

Let
[a]\rightarrow[b]
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. For a representative
(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f}):(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(j,b)
, we obtain a chain of composable morphisms
\psi(\bar{f})
in
\mathcal{D}
(in virtue of
\psi
being natural). We define

H(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f})=\circ(\psi(\bar{f}))

where the expression on the right denoted the composition of the chain.

This mapping is well-defined. Indeed, assume we have a 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}F

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime},\bar{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{l},\bar{r},\bar{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{j}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}}

In virtue of this cell being indexed by a cell
\rho:(\bar{l},\bar{r})\Rightarrow(\bar{l}^{\prime},\bar{r}^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}
, which is a natural transformation of zig-zags in
\mathcal{J}
, and the fact that
\bar{g}=\rho^{*}\bar{f}
, we must have
\circ(\psi(\bar{f}))=\circ(\psi(\bar{g}))
.

It is clear that
H
preserves composition and that
H\phi=\psi
. Hence the universality of
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Note

(The double category of elements)
.

In the previous section we mentioned how the colimit of a diagram of a sets
F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Set}
is isomorphic to the set of connected components
\pi_{0}\mathcal{E}(F)
of the category of elements of
F
. But, we did not follow up in categorifying this idea, even though we did so for the categories of zig-zags introduced in the same section.

One way to achieve such a categorification is by introducing the
double category of elements

\mathbb{E}(F)
associated to a diagram of categories
F:\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}
. This category is defined with:

(\bullet)

Set of objects
DC=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{J}}Ob(\mathcal{C}_{i})
.

(\downarrow)

Vertical category the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i\in\mathcal{J}}\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

(\mapsto)

A horizontal morphism
u^{*}:(i,a)\mapsto(j,a^{\prime})
being a morphism in the category of elements
\mathcal{E}(ObF)
, i.e. a morphism
u:i\rightarrow j
in
\mathcal{J}
such that
a\mapsto a^{\prime}
under
\tilde{u}
.

(\square)

2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{*}}

in case of a morphism
u:i\rightarrow j
in
\mathcal{J}
such that
\tilde{u}(f)=g
.

If we consider the category of elements as an attempt to depict a diagram of sets, the double category of elements displayed above is an attempt to depict a diagram of categories.

In general, for a double category
\mathbb{D}
we may define the
vertical category of connected components

\pi_{0}^{v}\mathbb{D}
. The functor

\pi_{0}^{v}:\textbf{DCat}\rightarrow\textbf{Cat}

from double categories to categories can be defined to be the left adjoint to the functor
\mathbb{V}:\textbf{Cat}\rightarrow\textbf{DCat}
which regards a category
\mathcal{A}
as a double category
\mathbb{V}\mathcal{A}
with
\mathcal{A}
in the vertical direction and the horizontal direction trivial.

For general double categories
\mathbb{D}
, it is certainly tedious to describe
\pi_{0}^{v}\mathbb{D}
explicitly, but we can understand from the universal property that any 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

provides an identification
a\sim b
,
c\sim d
and
f\sim g
.
For this reason, we understand that

\colim F\cong\pi_{0}^{v}\mathbb{E}(F)

In some sense, the double category
\mathbb{Z}F
is a way of symmetrizing
\mathbb{E}(F)
in order to have an explicit colimit.

4
The necklace theorem

Let
X
be a simplicial set.
We adopt the following conventions:

-

For an
n
-simplex
x\in X_{n}
, let
\Delta^{n}(x)
denote the copy of
\Delta^{n}
indexed by the simplex
x
.

-

V(x)
denotes the set of vertices of the simplex
\Delta^{n}(x)
. We regard it as an ordered set.

-

For vertices
a,b\in V(x)
we denote by
x_{\overrightarrow{ab}}
the subsimplex of
x
spanned by vertices “between”
a
and
b
.

Let
\mathcal{S}(X)
denote the category of simplices of
X
(as defined in the introduction). There is a forgetful functor

\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}(X)&\rightarrow&\textbf{sSet}\\
x&\mapsto&\Delta^{n}(x)\end{matrix}

and by general presheaf theory we have

X\cong\colim_{x\in X_{n}}\Delta^{n}(x)

In a similar fashion we denote
\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}(x)
the copy of the simplicial category
\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}
indexed by the simplex
x\in X_{n}
. As mentioned in the introduction, for a fixed
[p]\in\Delta
, the category of
\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}(x)_{p}
is defined with:

(\bullet)

Set of objects
V(x)
.

(\triangle)

Given
a,b\in V(x)
, a
p
-arrow
\overrightarrow{U}:a\rightarrow b
is a flag
U^{0}\subseteq\dots\subseteq U^{p}
of subsets of
V(x_{\overrightarrow{ab}})
such that
a,b\in U^{0}
.

(\circ)

Composition is provided by union of sets.

Fix some
[p]\in\Delta
. The category of
p
-arrows
\mathfrak{C}(X)_{p}
is the colimit of the diagram of categories

\begin{matrix}\chi_{p}:&\mathcal{S}(X)&\rightarrow&\textbf{Cat}\\
&x&\mapsto&\mathbb{\Delta^{n}}(x)_{p}\end{matrix}

Therefore, the general theory developed in the previous section to describe
Cat
-valued colimits applies. We interpret in the language of simplices.

First of all, on objects, we have

Ob(\mathfrak{C}(X)_{p})=X_{0}

In light of our notation, an object
a\in X_{0}
is represented by a pair
(x,a)
where
x\in X_{n}
and
a\in V(x)
.

The bookkeeping of indices for the above colimit is carried out by the double category
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{S}(X)
, which we interpret as follows. Consider a zig-zag
T:[n]\rightsquigarrow[m]
in
\Delta
, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{k}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n_{k-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}

Being a diagram in
\Delta
, this zig-zag can be realized as a simplicial set

|T|=\Delta^{n}+_{\Delta^{m_{1}}}\dots+_{\Delta^{m_{k-1}}}\Delta^{m}

Moreover, any 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}\Delta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

is represented by a morphism of simplicial sets
\rho:|T|\rightarrow|S|
(beware though, there are more simplicial maps
|T|\rightarrow|S|
than 2-cells as above).

In this vein, a zig-zag in
\mathcal{S}(X)
is a map of simplicial sets
|T|\rightarrow X
for some zig-zag
T
in
\Delta
, and a 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{S}(X)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|T|\rightarrow X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces|S|\rightarrow X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

is represented by a simplicial map over
X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|T|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|S|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

Again, there are more such commutative triangles than 2-cells as above.

Let
(|T|\rightarrow X):x\rightsquigarrow y
be a zig-zag in
\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{S}(X)
, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x=x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k}=y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}

A
\chi_{p}
-decoration of such a zig-zag consists of a choice of vertices
a\in V(x)
,
a_{i}\in V(y_{i})
and
b\in V(y)
together with a chain
\overrightarrow{U}_{0}:a\rightarrow a_{1},\dots,\overrightarrow{U}_{k}:a_{k}\rightarrow b
, where
\overrightarrow{U}_{i}
is a morphism in
\mathbb{\Delta^{n_{i}}}(x_{i})_{p}
.

Observe that, in constructing a decoration, we do not make use of the simplices
y_{i}
except in choosing the vertices
a_{i}
. Moreover, in constructing a morphism
\overrightarrow{U}_{i}:a_{i}\rightarrow a_{i+1}
, we only make use of the subsimplex of
x_{i}
spanned by the vertices between
a_{i}
and
a_{i+1}
. In some sense, we see the notion of
necklace
originating from this observation.

Definition 4.1
.

-

(Necklace) A necklace
N
is a zig-zag in
\Delta
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n_{k-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{k}}

where the maps
\alpha
and
\omega
indicate the initial and terminal vertex inclusions.

-

(Necklace in
X
). A necklace in
X
is a zig-zag in
\mathcal{S}(X)
of the form
|N|\rightarrow X
for some necklace
N
in
\mathcal{Z}\Delta
, say

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{k}}

-

(Proper decoration). A
\chi_{p}
-decorated zig-zag
(x,a)\rightsquigarrow(y,b)
is called proper if
a
is the initial vertex of
x
and
b
the terminal vertex of
y
.

The realization of a necklace
N:[n]\rightsquigarrow[m]
in
\Delta
may be written as

|N|=\Delta^{n}\vee\dots\vee\Delta^{m}

Hence, we recover the definition of necklace given by Dugger and Spivak in
[
2
]
.
We may define beads, joins and vertices as in the introduction.
The novelty in our treatment is only in interpreting necklaces as zig-zags, which allows us to use the double categorical framework developed earlier.

Let
\mathbb{N}_{p}X
denote the full sub-double category of
\mathbb{Z}\chi_{p}
in which the horizontal morphism are properly decorated necklaces. The following proposition is a combinatorial unpacking of the structure of
\mathbb{N}_{p}X
in the language of simplicial sets.

Proposition 4.1
.

(i)

Let
N
and
M
be two necklaces in
\Delta
, say
|N|=\Delta^{n_{0}}\vee\dots\vee\Delta^{n_{k}}
and
|M|=\Delta^{m_{0}}\vee\dots\vee\Delta^{m_{l}}
.

There is a bijection between the set of 2-cells
(\mathbb{Z}\Delta)_{1}(N,M)
and
the set of simplicial maps
|N|\rightarrow|M|
which restrict to maps
\Delta^{n_{0}}\rightarrow\Delta^{m_{0}}
and
\Delta^{n_{k}}\rightarrow\Delta^{m_{l}}
.

(ii)

Let
|N|\rightarrow X
be a necklace in
X
. There is a bijection between proper
\chi_{p}
-decorations of this necklace (when regarded as a zig-zag in
\mathcal{S}(X)
) and flags
\overrightarrow{U}=(U^{0}\subseteq\dots\subseteq U^{p})
of subsets of
V_{N}
such that
J_{N}\subseteq U^{0}
.

(iii)

There is a bijection between 2-cells in
\mathbb{N}_{p}X

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a,x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a,x^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(a,x^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(|N|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(|M|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{*}}

and commutative triangles in
\textbf{sSet}_{*,*}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|N|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|M|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{a,b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

such that
\rho(\overrightarrow{U})=\overrightarrow{V}
.

Proof.

(i)

Let

\phi:|N|\rightarrow|M|

be a morphism which restricts to maps
\phi_{0}:\Delta^{n_{0}}\rightarrow\Delta^{m_{0}}
and
\phi_{k}:\Delta^{n_{k}}\rightarrow\Delta^{m_{l}}
.

In general, for
i=0,\dots,k
, the map
\phi
restricts to a simplicial map
\phi_{i}:\Delta^{n_{i}}\rightarrow\Delta^{m_{j}}
for some
j
which depends on
i
. This gives rise to a morphism
\theta:[k]\rightarrow[l]
in
\Delta
.

The map
\theta
has to be surjective since
\theta(0)=0
and
\theta(k)=l
. Therefore, the maps
\phi_{i}
serve as components for a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}^{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Z}^{l}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

which, by definition, is a 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}\Delta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n_{k}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{l}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

The above steps are reversible, hence the desired bijection.

(ii)

This is clear, since any chain
\overrightarrow{U}
as above may be split along the joins to produce a proper decoration, and vice versa.

(iii)

2-cells
\rho^{*}
as in the statement are indexed by 2-cells
\rho
in
\mathbb{Z}\Delta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

By (i), such 2-cells are precisely simplicial maps
\rho:|N|\rightarrow|M|
which restrict in the first and last component.

However, the fact that
\rho
indexes
\rho^{*}
forces the restrictions on
x
and
y
to preserve the first and last vertex respectively. Hence, such a morphism
\rho
is just a morphism of bipointed simplicial sets over
X_{a,b}
. The fact that
\rho(\overrightarrow{U}_{*})=\overrightarrow{V}_{*}
follows by (ii).

Conversely, any bipointed simplicial map
\rho:|N|\rightarrow|M|
will automatically restrict to morphisms on
x
and
y
. Hence the bijection.

∎

Construction 4.1

(Necklace replacement functor)
.

We construct a functor

N_{*}:(\mathbb{Z}\chi_{p})_{1}\rightarrow(\mathbb{N}_{p}X)_{1}

Let
(|T|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U}_{*}):(x,a)\rightsquigarrow(y,b)
be an object of
\mathbb{Z}(\chi_{p})_{1}
(which is a horizontal morphism in
\mathbb{Z}\chi_{p}
). Say, the indexing zig-zag in
\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{S}(X)
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x=x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k}=y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}

and the decoration is given by vertices
a\in V(x)
,
a_{i}\in V(y_{i})
and
b\in V(y)
together with a chain of morphisms
\overrightarrow{U}_{0}:a\rightarrow a_{1},\dots,\overrightarrow{U}_{k}:a_{k-1}\rightarrow b
(
\overrightarrow{U}_{i}
is a morphism in the category
\mathbb{\Delta^{n_{i}}}(x_{i})_{p}
, the dimension of
x_{i}
being
n_{i}
).

There is a necklace
|N_{T}|\rightarrow X
, equipped with an inclusion 2-cell
\epsilon_{T}
in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{S}(X)
which is displayed by the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\overrightarrow{aa_{1}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,\overrightarrow{a_{1}a_{2}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{\overrightarrow{a_{k}b}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

It is clear that the decoration
\overrightarrow{U}_{*}
originates from the the necklace on top, so that we have a 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}\chi_{p}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(x_{\overrightarrow{aa_{1}}},a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(y_{\overrightarrow{a_{k}b}},b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(x,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(y,b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(|N_{T}|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U}_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(|T|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U}_{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{T}}

The construction
(|T|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U}_{*})\mapsto(|N_{T}|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U}_{*})
is clearly functorial.
Moreover, the 2-cells
\epsilon
as above display the functor
N_{*}
as the right adjoint to the inclusion
(\mathbb{N}_{p}X)_{1}\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}\chi_{p})_{1}
.

Theorem 4.1

(Dugger and Spivak,
[
2
]
)
.

Let
X
be a simplicial set and
a,b\in X_{0}
. A
p
-arrow,
[p]\in\Delta
, in the set
(\mathfrak{C}X)_{p}(a,b)
is represented by

-

A necklace
|N|\rightarrow X_{a,b}
.

-

A flag
\overrightarrow{U}=(U^{0}\subseteq\dots\subseteq U^{p})
of subsets of
V_{N}
such that
J_{N}\subseteq U^{0}
.

This data is subject to the following relation: two pairs
(|N|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U})
and
(|M|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{V})
are identified
in case there is a commutative triangle in
\textbf{sSet}_{*,*}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|N|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|M|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{a,b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

such that
\rho(\overrightarrow{U})=\overrightarrow{V}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.1
, a
p
-arrow
a\rightarrow b
in
\mathfrak{C}X
is represented by a
\chi_{p}
-decorated zig-zag
(|T|\rightarrow X,\overrightarrow{U}):(x,a)\rightsquigarrow(y,b)
. We may apply the necklace replacement functor defined in Construction
4.1
, so without loss of generality we may assume that
T
is a necklace and
\overrightarrow{U}
is a proper decoration.

The relation such data is subject to is recorded in the 2-cells of the double category
\mathbb{N}_{p}X
. By part (ii) of Proposition
4.1
, this relation is exactly the one stated in the theorem.
∎

Appendix A
Zig-zag gymnastics

A.1
Unital and transposition 2-cells

We describe a couple of maneuvers which are useful in working with the double categories
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F
.

Let us denote by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}

a zig-zag in
\mathcal{Z}\mathcal{J}
which is given by a constant functor
u_{n}:\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
at
i\in\mathcal{J}
. Sometimes we do not specify the length of the zig-zag, but we leave it as understood in context.

The trivial functor
\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}
induces a 2-cell in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{J}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{i}}

We may regard the above as a 2-cell in
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

In conclusion, we have 2-cells in
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}
of the form

\hbox to68.11pt{\vbox to48.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 34.05617pt\lower-25.27441pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where the cell on the right is obtained by symmetry.
In fact, the lengths of the vertical zig-zags may vary as we wish. We call 2-cells as above
unital
.

In
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}
, horizontal and vertical morphisms are the same: they are zig-zags in
\mathcal{J}
. Given a zig-zag
(\bar{u},\bar{v}):i\rightsquigarrow j
, there is a distinguished 2-cell of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{u},\bar{v})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{u},\bar{v})}

which we describe as follows.

In case of a zig-zag of length
1
, say
i\xleftarrow{u}k\xrightarrow{v}j
, the above 2-cell is given by the following commutative diagram in
\mathcal{J}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}

For longer zig-zags, we can proceed recursively.

Because of symmetry, we also have 2-cells of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{u},\bar{v})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{u},\bar{v})}

We call these two types of cells
transpositions
, since they transpose zig-zags from the vertical to the horizontal direction. In general, by applying unital and transposition 2-cells, any 2-cell in
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{J}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}

may be transposed to a 2-cell of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{i^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{j^{\prime}}}

In the double category
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F
, we denote by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}

a decoration of a trivial zig-zag
\mathcal{Z}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}
by identity morphisms
id_{a}
.

In
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F
, the vertical morphisms are
Ob(F)
-decorated, and hence we may regard them as horizontal morphisms decorated by identities. It is easy to check that the above unital and transposition 2-cells exist in
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F
as well.

A.2
Proof of Proposition
3.1

Lemma A.1
.

The morphism
id_{[a]}
is well-defined.

Proof.

Let
(i,a)
and
(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
be two representatives for
[a]
. There exist an
ObF
-decorated zig-zag
(a,i)\rightsquigarrow(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
, which we may regard as a 2-cell in
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F
and hence identifies
(\{i\},id_{a})
and
(\{i^{\prime}\},id_{a^{\prime}})

∎

Lemma A.2
.

Any
ObF
-decorated zig-zag
(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})
represents
id_{[a]}
.

Proof.

For a chosen
ObF
-decorated zig-zag, we have a 2-cell in
{\mathbb{Z}}F
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i^{\prime},a^{\prime})}}

The zig-zag on the bottom is evidently equivalent to
(\{i^{\prime}\},id_{a^{\prime}})
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

A morphism
[a]\rightarrow[b]
in
\mathcal{C}
represented by a
F
-decorated zig-zag
(i,a)\rightsquigarrow(j,b)
, is also represented by the composite of the latter with any
ObF
-decorated zig-zag
(j,b)\rightsquigarrow(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We have the following diagram in
\tilde{\mathbb{Z}}F

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}

∎

Lemma A.4
.

Given 2-cells in
{\mathbb{Z}}(F)

there is alsways a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i,a)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j,b)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c,k)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j^{\prime},b^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(c^{\prime},k^{\prime})}}

whose top and bottom are the composites of the tops and bottoms of the given 2-cells.

Moreover, by adjusting the lengths of the vertical identities, these 2-cells are composable. The desired conclusion follows from the previous lemma.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Composition in
\mathcal{C}
is well-defined.

Proof.

Let
[a]\rightarrow[b]
and
[b]\rightarrow[c]
be composable morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. Assume the composite morphism is represented by two triples composites of decorated zig-zags

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1},b_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{1},c_{1})}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{2},a_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{2},b_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{2},c_{2})}}

We have a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1},b_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{2},a_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{2},b_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime})}}

since by Lemma
A.3
, the top and bottom composites both represent
[a]\rightarrow[b]
. Similarly, we also have a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{1},c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{2},c_{2})}}

By Lemma
A.4
, there exist a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{1},a_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1},b_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{1}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{1},c_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i_{2},a_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{2},b_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(j_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(k_{2},c_{2})}}

which identifies the triple composites used in expressing the composition in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

It easily follows that composition in
\mathcal{C}
is unital and associative.
